## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides, Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government, the Provincial Coordinating Units in other provinces/areas; private sector/ civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- x Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- x Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- x Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- x Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- x AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated Progress Reports will be prepared by the Provincial/Area Coordination Units and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education indicators as well.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.

# Islamabad Capital Territory (ICT) Plan (2013-16)

## Situation Analysis 2011-12

The present education profile of ICT reflects a primary-age group (5-9 years) population of 143,962 children, of which 75,416 are boys and 68,546 are girls (Table 1a). Gross primary enrolment rate is 87%, with 85% for boys and 90% for girls while the net enrolment rate of 70% is much lower, with 68% for boys and 72% for girls.  It may be noted that among all provinces /areas in the country, ICT has higher enrolment rates for girls than boys. Completion of primary education is high, with only 10% students dropping out before passing primary level.

## ICT: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Strategies

8 In ICT, for the education sector, two key issues were identified :

- Lack of access to education; and

- Poor quality of education

For each of these, a set of in-school and out-of-school factors were identified, along with some solutions for overcoming these challenges. Following is a matrix of these issues, along with a set of factors and corresponding strategies:

Access
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Lack of structure and facilities for Pre-primary education Ÿ Lack of proper infrastructure and physical facilities. Ÿ Unavailability of budget for repair and maintenance of buildings Ÿ Lack of transport facilities. Ÿ Absence of proper Admission Policy | Ÿ ECE needs to be institutionalized in all Primary schools Ÿ Proper infrastructure and missing facilities need to be provided. Ÿ Annual budget for repair and maintenance for each school needs to be allocated. Ÿ Transport facilities need to be provided. Ÿ Admission Policy is required to be revised with clear indication of cluster level feeding institution/sector.
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Immigrants especially from KPK Ÿ Children from surrounding areas of Rawalpindi Ÿ Poverty Ÿ Parent Awareness | Ÿ Special non-formal schools need to be opened in the areas of Katchi Abadi. Ÿ Admission to the children of residents of Islamabad and Federal Government employees be restricted. Ÿ Incentives/scholarships need to be awarded to the needy students. Ÿ Community awareness campaign needs to be launched for admission of out of school children.

ICT:  Issues/Challenges and Suggested Solution

Quality
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Rationalization of Teachers (student teachers ratio needs to be rationalized ) Ÿ Lack of Math & Science qualified teachers Ÿ Delay in provision of text books Ÿ Assessment system is not aligned with new curriculum. Ÿ Inconsistency in medium of instruction at different levels (primary/ middle/ high ) Ÿ Teachers lacking aptitude and competencies. | Ÿ Deployment of teachers as per numbers of students enrolled and subject offered Ÿ Subject based qualified teachers be appointed. Ÿ Timely printing and provision of free text books. Ÿ Continuous assessment system needs to be adopted.

National Plan of Action ICT (2013-16)

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

Out-of-school Factors

- Ÿ Community participation in schools

- Ÿ Lack of monitoring and supervision

- Ÿ Development of Curriculum and Textbooks

- Ÿ Interference of politician and unions.

- Ÿ Medium of instruction needs to be decided and adopted for all public and private institutions.

- Ÿ Competency based In-service Training to be provided to all teachers on periodic basis.

Strategies/Interventions

- Ÿ Role and function of PTAs need to be strengthened with provision of annual budget.

- Ÿ The structure and functioning of AEOs needs to be further strengthened.

- Ÿ Timely provision of textbooks

- Ÿ Educational administration needs to be strengthened with power delegation and support to deal such interference.

Governance
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Lack of School Mapping in view of catchment area and population density Ÿ Transfer/posting of teachers Ÿ Placement of teacher on need based/Class enrolment Ÿ Culture of temporary duty | Ÿ Establishment of new educational institutions purely on need basis Ÿ Transfer/Posting policies and implementation mechanism Ÿ Need rationalization of posting of teachers

In light of the above issues, factors and suggested strategies, the ICT  Education Plan (2013-16) has been developed, focusing on: (i)  bringing  in  maximum  number  of  primary-age  out-of-school  children  to  be enrolled in formal and non-formal schools through expansion of schools, awareness campaigns, etc (ii) increase  retention  in  primary  grades  through  provision  of  proper  teaching-learning  environment, textbooks, other incentives, etc., (iii) improve quality of education through teachers' training, community participation, etc. and (iv) specific provision of other incentives (e.g. stipends, food for education, uniforms, etc) to retain children from most disadvantaged/rural/remote areas, especially girls.

The four specific strategies identified (and budgeted) under Plan include:

- Enrolment of new students in existing schools: This strategy focuses on enrolling out-of-school children in existing primary schools with underutilized capacity. As admission policy does not allow children aged 7+ years in formal primary classes, over-age children will be enrolled in non-formal basic education, feeder schools or religious (deeni madrassah) schools.

- Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room: In existing public formal schools, where extra space is available, construction of an additional classroom or academic block and provision of a teacher will help in accommodating a number of new students.

- Enrolment of new students in new schools: ICT does not plan to construct any new formal public school.

- Targeted  Incentives: This strategy is aimed  at  retaining students, especially  those  from disadvantaged  groups,  especially  girls,  who  (mainly  due  to  financial  reasons)  drop  out  before completing primary school. These incentives (e.g. stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc.) will be given to students from poor families

105

National Plan of Action ICT (2013-16)

[[END OF PAGE 110]]

[[START OF PAGE 111]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

The key assumptions of the ICT Education Plan are:

- Using  actual  estimates  of  population  for  the  year  2012  and  net  primary  enrolment  rates  for 2011/12, projections for these variables were made for the base year 2012/13 and targets set for the Plan period 2013-16;

- The Plan is based on annual physical targets and corresponding estimated costs for a three year period i.e. 2013/14-15/16;

- For each strategy, a certain proportion of out-of-school children to be enrolled is specified;

- There are basically four types of schools assigned to absorb additional enrolment i.e., public sector formal schools; non-formal basic and feeder schools; religious or deeni madaris institutions; and private sector schools;

- Costs of additional enrolments are estimated for each type of school except private sector schools which are not financed by the public treasury;

- Under each strategy, recurrent costs are estimated at the rate of  Rs 20,000 per student in formal public schools and Rs 5,000 per student in non-formal/feeder /deeni madrassah;

- Plan estimates are based on annual inflation rate of 10%;

- A student-teacher ratio of 30:1; and a teacher-administrator/supervisor ratio of 40:1 is assumed.

- For quality enhancement (e.g., teacher training, curriculum reform, provision of missing facilities, etc.), 15% of recurrent costs are added to the total cost.

- For innovative strategies (e.g., awareness campaigns to promote the importance of education, community involvement in supervision of local education; and use of media; etc.), an amount equivalent to 15% of recurrent costs plus quality enhancement costs is added to total costs.

- To improve access and retention, incentives such as stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc. will  be  given  to  children  form  disadvantaged  (rural/backward/remote)  areas  and  belonging  to disadvantaged groups (especially girls) in selected area of the districts. These are budgeted at a rate of Rs 3,000 per student per year.

- A contingency amount equivalent to 10% of all development, recurrent, quality and innovative strategies' costs is added to cover miscellaneous and unforeseen expenditures.

## Physical Targets and Cost Estimates for Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs
### Strategies to Increase New Enrolment and Enhance Quality in ICT

Within the Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs plan, a total number of 46,869 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in ICT, under four possible mutually exclusive strategies:

107

#### Strategy (a)

##### Enrolment of new students in existing schools (under-utilized) (50% to 60% of all new enrolment)

A major strategy to be implemented under the Plan suggests enrolment of out-of-school children in primary classes of existing formal schools. A careful assessment of schools with underutilized capacity as well as nonfunctional schools will have to be undertaken before allocating new students to these institutions.

Under the strategy of enrolling new students in existing schools, a total number of 26,335 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in ICT during 2013-16. Of these, 50% will be enrolled in public formal schools and another 10% is expected to be absorbed by the private sector schools. (Table ISa1). ICT does not plan to expand enrolment in non-formal schools.

#### Strategy (b)

##### Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room (In 15% of existing schools)

This strategy is based on the construction of one additional classroom (estimated at a cost of Rs 1 million) and hiring  of  one  teacher  in  an  existing  public  school.  An  assessment  of  schools  with  extra  land  and possibility of enrolling out-of-school students will have to be carefully undertaken.

Of the total new students to be enrolled in primary classes in ICT during 2013-16, 15% i.e., 2,679 students will be accommodated in formal public schools which will be provided with an additional classroom and one additional teacher. Assuming 30 students per classroom, a total of 89 additional rooms (and 89 teachers) will be provided in 89 formal schools (Table Isb1).

## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides, Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government, the Provincial Coordinating Units in other provinces/areas; private sector/ civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- Ÿ AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated Progress Reports will be prepared by the Provincial/Area Coordination Units and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education indicators as well.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.

# Khyber Pakhtunkhwa Plan of Action

## Situation Analysis 2011-12

The present education profile of Khyber Pakhtunkhwa reflects a primary-age group (5-9 years) population of 2.8 million children, of which 1.47 million are boys and 1.37 million are girls (Table 1a). The net enrolment rate  of  81%  is  very  low,  with  93%  for  boys  and  69%  for  girls.  Due  to  poverty,  poor  teaching-learning conditions in school, and the child being required to help at home or work, drop-out rates are high with onethird of children leaving school before completing primary education.

## KP: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Strategies

9 In KP, for the education sector, two key issues were identified :

- Lack of access to education; and

- Poor quality of education

For each of these, a set of in-school and out-of-school factors were identified, along with some solutions for overcoming  these  challenges.  Following  is  a  matrix  of  these  issues,  along  with  a  set  of  factors  and corresponding strategies:

Access
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Teacher absenteeism Ÿ Missing basic facilities Ÿ Lack of friendly environment Ÿ Teachers' harsh attitude | Ÿ Strict supervision teacher absenteeism & Provision of transport to teachers Ÿ Provision of basic facilities Ÿ No corporal punishment
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Distance especially for females Ÿ Insecurity Ÿ Poverty Ÿ Culture/Nomadic factors Ÿ Parents are reluctant Ÿ Parents lack awareness | Ÿ New schools to be established nearer to communities Ÿ NFE/Community/Feeder schools be established Ÿ Social mobilization in favour of education/awareness campaigns Ÿ Target setting for teachers and PTC members
Quality
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Ill Trained Teachers Ÿ Outdated Teaching Methods Ÿ Missing facilities Ÿ Lack of Monitoring/Supervision | Ÿ Training of Teachers/refresher courses Ÿ Teaching methods to be revised Ÿ Provision of basic facilities Ÿ Involve community members to monitor schools
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Poor Governance Ÿ Political pressures | Ÿ Effective monitoring and supervision Ÿ Rewards and punishments Ÿ Involve community members to monitor schools Ÿ End political interference

KP: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Solution

In light of the above issues, factors and suggested strategies, the Khyber Pakhtunkhwa   education Plan (2013-16)  was  developed,  focusing  on:  (i)  bringing  in  maximum  number  of  primary-age  out-of-school children  to  be  enrolled  in  formal  and  non-formal  schools  through  provision/expansion  of  schools, awareness campaigns, etc (ii) increase retention in primary grades through provision of proper teachinglearning environment, textbooks, other incentives, etc., (iii) improve quality of education through teachers'

> 9 In an Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs (KP) workshop held in Peshawar on April 18, 2013 which was attended by Federal and Provincial education officials, representatives of the civil society and private sector, international development partners and education experts.

National Plan of Action KPK (2013-16)

124

[[END OF PAGE 129]]

[[START OF PAGE 130]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

training, community participation, etc. and (iv) specific provision of other incentives (e.g. stipends, food for education, uniforms, etc) to retain children from most disadvantaged/rural/remote areas, especially girls.

The four specific strategies identified (and budgeted) under  Plan include:

- Enrolment of new students in existing schools: This strategy focuses on enrolling out-of-school children in existing primary schools with underutilized capacity.   Also, there are several closed schools which can be made functional.  As admission policy does not allow children aged 7+ years in formal  primary  classes,  over-age  children  will  be  enrolled  in  non-formal  basic  education, community feeder or religious (deeni madrassah) schools.

- Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room: In existing public formal schools, where extra space is available, construction of an additional classroom and provision of a teacher will help in accommodating a number of new students.

- Enrolment of new students in new schools: In disadvantaged/remote areas where enrolment is low due to non-availability of formal public schools, construction of two-room new formal and opening of one room non formal schools will help in improving access to education.

- Targeted  Incentives:  This  strategy  is  aimed  at  retaining  students,  particularly  those  from disadvantaged  groups,  especially  girls,  who  (mainly  due  to  financial  reasons)  drop  out  before completing primary school. These incentives (e.g. stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc.) will be given to students from poor families.

The key assumptions of  the  KP  Education Plan are:

- Using  actual  estimates  of  population  for  the  year  2012  and  net  primary  enrolment  rates  for 2011/12, projections for these variables were made for the base year 2012/13 and targets set for the Plan period 2013-16;

- The Plan is based on annual physical targets and corresponding estimated costs for a three year period i.e. 2013-16;

- For each strategy, a certain proportion of out-of-school children to be enrolled is specified;

- There are basically four types of schools assigned to absorb additional enrolment i.e., public sector formal schools; non-formal basic and feeder schools; religious or deeni madaris institutions; and private sector schools;

- Costs of additional enrolments are estimated for each type of school except private sector schools which are not financed by the public treasury;

- Under each strategy, besides specified development costs (i.e., Rs 1 million per additional room and Rs 3 million for a 2-room school in the public sector; and Rs 100,000 per new non formal school) , recurrent costs are estimated at the rate of  Rs 10,000 per student in formal public schools and Rs 5,000 per student in non-formal/feeder /deeni madrassah;

- Plan estimates are based on annual inflation rate of 10%;

- A student-teacher ratio of 30:1; and a teacher-administrator/supervisor ratio of 40:1 is assumed.

- For quality enhancement (e.g., teacher training, curriculum reform, provision of missing facilities, etc.), 20% of recurrent costs are added to the total cost.

- For innovative strategies (e.g., awareness campaigns to promote the importance of education, community involvement in supervision of local education; and use of media; etc.), an amount equivalent to 15% of recurrent costs plus quality enhancement costs is added to total costs.

125

National Plan of Action KPK (2013-16)

[[END OF PAGE 130]]

[[START OF PAGE 131]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

- To improve access and retention, incentives such as stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc. will be targeted towards children in disadvantaged (rural/backward/remote) areas and belonging to disadvantaged groups (especially girls) in selected poor districts. This would be costed at Rs 3,000 per student per year.

A contingency amount equivalent to 10% of all development, recurrent, quality and innovative strategies' costs is added to cover miscellaneous and unforeseen heads.

## Physical Targets and Cost Estimates for Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs
### Strategies to Increase  New  Enrolment  and  Enhance  Quality  in  Khyber Pakhtunkhwa

Within the Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs plan, a total number of 449,144 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in KP, under four possible mutually exclusive strategies:

#### Strategy (a)

##### Enrolment of new students in existing schools (under-utilized/closed schools) (50% of all new enrolment)

A major strategy to be implemented under the Plan suggests enrolment of out-of-school children in primary classes  of  existing  formal  and  non-formal  schools.  A  careful  assessment  of  schools  with  underutilized capacity as well as nonfunctional/closed schools will have to be undertaken before allocating new students to these institutions.

Under the strategy of enrolling new students in existing schools, a total number of 224,572 (50% of the total) new students will be enrolled in primary classes in KP during 2013-16. Of these, 25% will be enrolled in public formal and 5% in public non formal schools and 10% in deeni madaris and other schools.  Private schools are estimated to absorb another 10% students (Table KSa1).

## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the  National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Units  in  other  provinces/areas;  private  sector/  civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- Ÿ AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated  Progress  Reports  will  be  prepared  by  the  Provincial/Area  Coordination  Units  and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose, monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education measurement.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.

# Punjab Plan of Action

## Situation Analysis 2011-12

The present education profile of Punjab reflects primary-age group (5-9 years) population of 10.8 million children, of which 5.6 million are boys and 5.2 million are girls (Table 1a). Gross primary enrolment rate is 88%, with 91% for boys and 84% for girls while the net enrolment rate of 70% is much lower, with 73% for boys and 67% for girls. Due to poverty, poor teaching-learning conditions in school, and the child being required to help at home or work, drop-out rates are high with one-quarter of children leave school before completing primary education.

## Punjab: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Strategies

11 In Punjab, for the education sector, two key issues were identified :

- Lack of access to education; and

- Poor quality of education

For each of these, a set of in-school and out-of-school factors were identified, along with some solutions for overcoming  these  challenges.  Following  is  a  matrix  of  these  issues,  along  with  a  set  of  factors  and corresponding strategies:

Access
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Lack of adequate facilities and services in schools. Ÿ Non rationalization for the existing facilities. i.e. over utilized and underutilized schools need to be rationalized (2.3 million out-of- school children can be adjusted with existing facilities, currently 1:18 teacher learner ratio does exist) Ÿ Non consideration of Building codes for educational building construction (including Disaster Risk Reduction measures) Ÿ Lack of teachers' availability and commitment. Ÿ Non conducive school environment and missing facilities Ÿ Corporal punishment Ÿ Allocation of Budget (non-salary) on per student cost Ÿ Bad governance/ Lack of professionalism of teachers Ÿ Reduced red-tapism from the top to improve education Ÿ Lack of parent teachers interaction Ÿ Lack of administrative capacity of the school management, monitoring & evaluation. Ÿ Lack of strength of school management committees | Ÿ Provision of standardized physical facilities and services in schools Ÿ Merger, consolidation and rationalization of schools. Ÿ Modification/renovation of school designs for DRR measures. Ÿ Training and capacity building of the teachers. Ÿ Child friendly education at schools Ÿ Retention of students through allocating a unique number to every enrolled child. Ÿ Ensure Birth registration of every child. Ÿ No relaxation on corporal punishment Ÿ Boarding facilities at tehsil levels to students with zero liability on the parents. Ÿ Introduction of stipend for the poor and girl children to address child labor. Ÿ Inclusiveness of special children (disabled and mentally challenged etc.) Ÿ Implementation of effective and regular parents teachers meeting

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

In light of the above issues, factors and suggested strategies, the Punjab   education Plan (2013-16) was developed, focusing on : (i) bringing in maximum number of primary-aged out-of-school children to be enrolled in formal and non-formal schools through provision/expansion of schools, awareness campaigns, etc (ii) increase retention in primary grades through provision of proper teaching-learning environment, textbooks, other incentives, etc., (iii) improve quality of education through teachers' training, community participation, etc. and (iv) specific provision of other incentives (e.g. stipends, food for education, uniforms, etc) to retain children from most disadvantaged/rural/remote areas, especially girls.

The four specific strategies identified (and budgeted) under Plan include:

- Enrolment of new students in existing schools: This strategy focuses on enrolling out-of-school children in existing primary schools with underutilized capacity.   Also, there are several closed schools which can be made functional.  As admission policy does not allow children aged 7+ years in formal  primary  classes,  over-age  children  will  be  enrolled  in  non-formal  basic  education, community feeder or religious (deeni madrassah) schools.

- Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room: In existing public formal schools, where extra space is available, construction of an additional classroom and provision of a teacher will help in accommodating a number of new students.

- Enrolment of new students in new schools: In disadvantaged/remote areas where enrolment is low due to non-availability of formal public schools, construction of two-room new formal and opening of one room non-formal schools will help in improving access to education.

- Targeted  Incentives:  This  strategy  is  aimed  at  retaining  students,  particularly  those  from disadvantaged  groups,  especially  girls,  who  (mainly  due  to  financial  reasons)  drop  out  before completing primary school. These incentives (e.g. stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc.) will be given to students from poor families.

The key assumptions of  the  Punjab Education Plan are:

- Using actual estimates of population for the year 2012 and net primary enrolment rates for2011-12, projections for these variables were made for the base year 2012-13 and targets set for the Plan period 2013-16;

- The Plan is based on annual physical targets and corresponding estimated costs for a three year period i.e. 201316;

- For each strategy, a certain proportion of out-of-school children to be enrolled is specified;

- There are basically four types of schools assigned to absorb additional enrolment i.e., public sector formal schools; non-formal basic and feeder schools; religious or deeni madaris institutions; and private sector schools;

- Costs of additional enrolments are estimated for each type of school except private sector schools which are not financed by the public treasury;

- Under each strategy, besides specified development costs (i.e., Rs 1 million per additional room and Rs 3 million for a 2-room school in the public sector; and Rs 100,000 per new non-formal school) , recurrent costs are estimated at the rate of  Rs 10,000 per student in formal public schools and Rs 5,000 per student in non-formal/feeder /deeni madrassah;

- Plan estimates are based on annual inflation rate of 10%;

- A student-teacher ratio of 30:1; and a teacher-administrator/supervisor ratio of 40:1 is assumed.

- For quality enhancement (e.g., teacher training, curriculum reform, provision of missing facilities,

National Plan of Action Punjab (2013-16)

146

[[END OF PAGE 151]]

[[START OF PAGE 152]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

- etc.), 20% of recurrent costs are added to the total cost.

- For innovative strategies (e.g., awareness campaigns to promote the importance of education, community involvement in supervision of local education; and use of media; etc.), an amount equivalent to 15% of recurrent costs plus quality enhancement costs is added to total costs.

- To improve access and retention, incentives such as stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc. will be targeted towards children in disadvantaged (rural/backward/remote) areas and belonging to disadvantaged groups (especially girls) in selected poor districts. This would be costed at Rs 3,000 per student per year.

- A contingency amount equivalent to 10% of all development, recurrent, quality and innovative strategies' costs is added to cover miscellaneous and unforeseen heads.

## Punjab Plan (2013-16)

According to most recent projections, the population of primary-age children (5-9 years) in Punjab is likely to gradually rise to 11.2 million by 2015/16, of which 5.76 million will be boys and 5.4 million will be girls (Table P1a).

## Physical Targets and Cost Estimates for Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs
### Strategies to Increase New Enrolment and Enhance Quality in Punjab

Within the Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs plan, a total number of 3,337,678 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in Punjab, under four possible mutually exclusive strategies:

#### Strategy (a)

##### Enrolment of new students in existing schools (under-utilized/closed schools) (70% of all new enrolment)

A major strategy to be implemented under the Plan suggests enrolment of out-of-school children in primary classes  of  existing  formal  and  non-formal  schools.  A  careful  assessment  of  schools  with  underutilized capacity as well as nonfunctional/closed schools will have to be undertaken before allocating new students to these institutions.

Under the strategy of enrolling new students in existing schools, a total number of 2,336,375 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in Punjab during 2013-16. Of these, 40% will be enrolled in public formal and 10% in public non formal schools and 10% in deeni madaris and other schools. Private schools are estimated to absorb another 10% students (Table PS1a).

#### Strategy (b)

##### Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room (In 10% of existing schools)

This strategy is based on the construction of one additional classroom (estimated at a cost of Rs 1 million) and hiring  of  one  teacher  in  an  existing  public  school.  An  assessment  of  schools  with  extra  land  and possibility of enrolling out-of-school students will have to be carefully undertaken.

Of the total number of 3,337,678 new students to be enrolled in primary classes in Punjab during 2013-16, 10% i.e., 333,768 students will be accommodated in formal public schools which will be provided with an additional classroom and one additional teacher. Assuming 30 students per classroom, a total of 11,126 additional rooms (and 11,126 teachers) will be provided in 11,126 formal schools (Table PSb1).

#### Strategy (d)

##### Targeted Incentives for access and retention for disadvantaged groups and girls (15% of Gross Enrolments)

Of all primary enrolments (old and new), 15% of the students will be provided incentives for access and retention in primary schools. In Punjab, 5.6 million children will be targeted in 3 years and provided incentives (equivalent to Rs. 3,000 per child) at a cost of Rs. 16.9 billion (Tables PSd1 and PSd2).

## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the  National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Units  in  other  provinces/areas;  private  sector/  civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- Ÿ AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated  Progress  Reports  will  be  prepared  by  the  Provincial/Area  Coordination  Units  and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose, monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education measurement.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.

# Sindh Plan of Action

## Situation Analysis 2011-12

The  present  education  profile  in  Sindh  reflects  primary-age  group  (5-9  years)  population  of  5  million children, of which 2.64 million are boys and 2.38 million are girls (Table 1a). Gross primary enrolment rate is 79%, with 86% for boys and 71% for girls while the net enrolment rate of 63% is much lower, with 67% for boys and 57% for girls. Due to poverty, poor teaching-learning conditions in school, and the child being required to help at home or work, drop-out rates are high with one-third of children leaving school before completing primary education.

## Sindh: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Strategies

14 In Sindh, for the education sector, two key issues were identified :

- Lack of access to education; and

- Poor quality of education

For each of these, a set of in-school and out-of-school factors were identified, along with some solutions for overcoming  these  challenges.  Following  is  a  matrix  of  these  issues,  along  with  a  set  of  factors  and corresponding strategies:

Sindh: Issues/Challenges and Suggested Solution

### Access
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ School fees and cost of books, stationery, uniforms and other costs, inhibit the enrolment of children from poor families Ÿ Schools lack basic infrastructure such as water, toilets, electricity Ÿ Children leaving schools dropping out/ without completion due to poor learning conditions in schools Ÿ School buildings need repair | Ÿ Provision of complete free education Ÿ Provision of incentives e.g. stipend, food for education, uniform etc. Ÿ Provision of basic infrastructure and facilities
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Social attitudes and lack of social mobility neither encourage nor see the need for education, especially for girls Ÿ Conservatism and illiteracy of parents Ÿ Distance from school- the further a child lives from school, the less likely he/she is to attend, especially for girls Ÿ Security concerns, especially with girls as also there are fewer schools for girls Ÿ Devastating earthquake, floods and terrorism has reduced the number of functional schools | Ÿ Awareness Campaigns on the importance of education, especially for girls; Ÿ Literacy programmes for parents/adults Ÿ Establishment of schools at safe and close proximity Ÿ Rehabilitation of devastated schools

> 14 In an Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs (Sindh) workshop held in Karachi on April 22, 2013 which was attended by Federal and Provincial education officials, representatives of the civil society and private sector, international development partners and education experts.

165

National Plan of Action Sindh (2013-16)

[[END OF PAGE 170]]

[[START OF PAGE 171]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

### Poor Quality of Education
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Teacher-student ratio is 1:30- too high for suitable learning environment Ÿ Multi grade teaching causes discipline and quality of teaching to suffer Ÿ No regular supervision/monitoring Ÿ Non-functional schools | Ÿ Recruitment of more teachers; Ÿ Better training of teachers in handling multi- grade teachings Ÿ Better supervision of schools and involvement of community in monitoring and supervising schools
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Family poverty & children need to contribute to household income so have no time for homework | Ÿ Provision of stipends to students who drop out due to work

In light of the above issues, factors and suggested strategies, the Sindh   education Plan (2013-16) was developed, focusing on: (i) bringing in maximum number of primary-aged out-of-school children to be enrolled in formal and non-formal schools through provision/expansion of schools, awareness campaigns, etc (ii) increase retention in primary grades through provision of proper teaching-learning environment, textbooks, other incentives, etc., (iii) improve quality of education through teachers' training, community participation, etc. and (iv) specific provision of other incentives (e.g. stipends, food for education, uniforms, etc) to retain children from most disadvantaged/rural/remote areas, especially girls.

The four specific strategies identified (and budgeted) under Plan include:

- Enrolment of new students in existing schools: This strategy focuses on enrolling out-of-school children in existing primary schools with underutilized capacity.   Also, there are several closed schools which can be made functional.  As admission policy does not allow children aged 7+ years in formal  primary  classes,  over-age  children  will  be  enrolled  in  non-formal  basic  education, community feeder or religious (deeni madrassah) schools.

- Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room: In existing public formal schools, where extra space is available, construction of an additional classroom and provision of a teacher will help in accommodating a number of new students.

- Enrolment of new students in new schools: In disadvantaged/remote areas where enrolment is low due to non-availability of formal public schools, construction of two-room new formal and opening of one room non formal schools will help in improving access to education.

- Targeted  Incentives: This strategy is aimed  at  retaining students, especially  those  from disadvantaged  groups,  especially  girls,  who  (mainly  due  to  financial  reasons)  drop  out  before completing primary school. These incentives (e.g. stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc.) will be given to students from poor families.

The key assumptions of  the  Sindh Education Plan are:

- Using  actual  estimates  of  population  for  the  year  2012  and  net  primary  enrolment  rates  for 2011/12, projections for these variables were made for the base year 2012/13 and for the Plan period 2013-16;

- The Plan is based on annual physical targets and corresponding estimated costs for a three year period i.e. 2013-14-15-16;

- For each strategy, a certain proportion of out-of-school children to be enrolled is specified;

National Plan of Action Sindh (2013-16)

166

[[END OF PAGE 171]]

[[START OF PAGE 172]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

- There are basically four types of schools assigned to absorb additional enrolment i.e., public sector formal schools; non-formal basic and feeder schools; religious or deeni madaris institutions; and private sector schools;

- Costs of additional enrolments are estimated for each type of school except private sector schools which are not financed by the public treasury;

- Under each strategy, besides specified development costs (i.e., Rs 1 million per additional room and Rs 3 million for a 2-room school in the public sector; and Rs 100,000 per new non formal school) , recurrent costs are estimated at the rate of  Rs 10,000 per student in formal public schools and Rs 5,000 per student in non-formal/feeder /deeni madrassah;

- Plan estimates are based on annual inflation rate of 10%;

- A student-teacher ratio of 30:1; and a teacher-administrator/supervisor ratio of 40:1 is assumed.

- For quality enhancement (e.g., teacher training, curriculum reform, provision of missing facilities, etc.), 20% of recurrent costs are added to the total cost.

- For innovative strategies (e.g., awareness campaigns to promote the importance of education, community involvement in supervision of local education; and use of media; etc.), an amount equivalent to 15% of recurrent costs plus quality enhancement costs is added to total costs.

- To improve access and retention, incentives such as stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc. will be targeted towards children in disadvantaged (rural/backward/remote) areas and belonging to disadvantaged groups (especially girls) in selected poor districts. These incentives are costed for an equivalent of 15% of newly enrolled children at Rs 3,000 per student per year.

- A contingency amount equivalent to 10% of all development, recurrent, quality and innovative strategies' costs is added to cover miscellaneous and unforeseen heads.

## Physical Targets and Cost Estimates for Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs
### Strategies to Increase New Enrolment and Enhance Quality in Sindh

Within the Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs plan, a total number of 897,941 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in Sindh, under four possible mutually exclusive strategies:

#### Strategy (a)

##### Enrolment of new students in existing schools (under-utilized/closed schools) (50% of all new enrolment)

A  major strategy to be implemented under the Plan suggests enrolment of out-of-school children in primary classes  of  existing  formal  and  non-formal  schools.  A  careful  assessment  of  schools  with  underutilized capacity as well as nonfunctional/closed schools will have to be undertaken before allocating new students to these institutions.

Under the strategy of enrolling new students in existing schools, a total number of 448,970 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in Sindh during 2013-16. Of these, 25% will be enrolled in public formal and 5% in public non-formal schools and 10% in deeni madaris and other schools.   Private schools are estimated to absorb another 10% students (Table Ss1a).

## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the  National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Units  in  other  provinces/areas;  private  sector/  civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- Ÿ AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated  Progress  Reports  will  be  prepared  by  the  Provincial/Area  Coordination  Units  and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose, monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education measurement.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.

# Azad Jammu and Kashmir (AJ&K) Plan of Action

## Situation Analysis 2011-12

The present education profile of AJ&K reflects a primary-age group (5-9 years) population   of 557,440 children, of which 286,210 are boys and 271,230 are girls (Table 1a). Gross primary enrolment rate is 73% for both boys and girls while the net enrolment rate of 58% is much lower, with 58% for boys and 59% for girls. Due to poverty, poor teaching-learning conditions in school, and the child being required to help at home or work, drop-out rates show that 13% of children leaving school before completing primary education.

## Issues/Challenges and Suggested Strategies

17 In AJ&K, for the education sector, two key issues were identified :

- Lack of access to education; and

- Poor quality of education

For each of these, a set of in-school and out-of-school factors were identified, along with some solutions for overcoming  these  challenges.  Following  is  a  matrix  of  these  issues,  along  with  a  set  of  factors  and corresponding strategies:

### Access
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Teacher : Ÿ Teacher Absenteeism School building : Ÿ Majority of schools are shelter less Ÿ Lack of space in existing schools Ÿ Lack of school furniture Ÿ Schools lack water sanitation facilities Ÿ Absence of playing ground and boundary wall in female schools Ÿ School design are not adopting DRR concept Schools supplies Ÿ Lack of Teaching kits Ÿ No free text book Other Ÿ Lack of DRR concept in curriculum Ÿ Lack of incentives and motivation of students. | Ÿ Teacher: Ÿ Check teacher absenteeism Ÿ Rationalization of teachers School building : Ÿ Convert shelter less schools in to proper sheltered schools Ÿ Allocation of sufficient funds/budget for repair and maintenance of schools and playgrounds Ÿ Repair and provision of water sanitation, hygiene facilities and electricity Ÿ DRR measures to be introduced to the degree possible in existing structures Schools supplies Ÿ Ensue provision of free text books Other Ÿ Inclusions of DRR in curriculum
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Policy : Ÿ No legislation on free and compulsory education Community Ÿ Lack of community involvement Ÿ Myth of education being not profitable Parent Ÿ Lack of Awareness of parents Ÿ Illiteracy Ÿ Poverty Geographic location Ÿ Hard and harsh weather conditions such as snow fall land sliding, flooded streams | Policy : Ÿ Introduce legislation of free and compulsory education Community Ÿ Increase community involvement by strengthening SMC Ÿ Launching of massive adult literacy program Ÿ Establish teachers and parent feedback mechanism Ÿ Feeder schools in far flung areas Parent Ÿ Increase awareness of parents Geographic location Ÿ Establishment of feeder schools in hard areas

### Quality
--- | ---
In School Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Teachers : Ÿ No merit in teacher recruitment Ÿ Irrational student teachers ratio Ÿ Inadequate training of teachers on curriculum. Ÿ Lack of qualified/trained teachers (Maths/Science/English teachers) Ÿ Corporal punishment Ÿ Lack of inclusive approach Curriculum and Textbooks: Ÿ Teachers not trained in new curriculum Ÿ Delay in provision of textbooks Ÿ Assessment system is not aligned with new curriculum. Ÿ Inconsistency in medium of instruction at different levels (primary/ middle/ high ) Infrastructure : Ÿ · Improper infrastructure/physical facilities (limited number of class rooms to accommodate more enrolment) Ÿ Missing facilities Improper monitoring and supervision Ÿ Lack of monitoring and supervision system Ÿ Inefficient Head teacher Ÿ Lack of accountability | Teachers Ÿ Merit-based appointment of teachers and ratio rationalization Ÿ Appointment of teachers as per numbers of students enrolled and subject offered Ÿ Regular Teachers' training. Ÿ Performance of teachers should be supervised. Ÿ Training of teachers on inclusive approach Ÿ Appointment of Trained teachers. Curriculum & Textbooks Ÿ Training of Teachers on new curriculum/ textbooks. Ÿ Timely provision of textbooks Ÿ Harmonize exams with new curriculum Ÿ Harmonize of medium of instruction Infrastructure Ÿ Improve infrastructure Ÿ Provide missing facilities Monitoring and Supervision Ÿ Arrange and strengthen PTAs/SMCs Ÿ School improvement plan should be developed and executed Ÿ CPDs should be ensured Ÿ Free and fair accountability
Out-of-school Factors | Strategies/Interventions
Ÿ Social factor such as broken families Ÿ Geographical factor Ÿ Environmental factor Ÿ Illiterate parents. Ÿ Economic factor (part time child worker ) Ÿ Poverty/child labour. | Ÿ Counseling of father/guardian (by PTA, community elders ) Ÿ Awareness of community and teachers Ÿ Incentives such as stipends to stop child labour

In light of the above issues, factors and suggested strategies, the AJ&K   Education Plan (2013-16) was developed, focusing on : (i) bringing in maximum number of primary-age group out-of-school children to be enrolled in formal and non-formal schools through provision/expansion of schools, awareness campaigns, etc (ii) increase retention in primary grades through provision of proper teaching-learning environment, textbooks, other incentives, etc., (iii) improve quality of education through teachers' training, community participation, etc. and (iv) specific provision of other incentives (e.g. stipends, food for education, uniforms, etc) to retain children from most disadvantaged/rural/remote areas, especially girls.

The four specific strategies identified (and budgeted) under Plan include:

- Enrolment of new students in existing schools: This strategy focuses on enrolling out-of-school children in existing primary schools with underutilized capacity.   Also, there are several closed schools which can be made functional.  As admission policy does not allow children aged 7+ years in formal  primary  classes,  over-age  children  will  be  enrolled  in  non-formal  basic  education,

National Plan of Action AJK (2013-16)

186

[[END OF PAGE 191]]

[[START OF PAGE 192]]

NPA to Accelerate Education-Related MDGs

community feeder or religious (deeni madrassah) schools.

- Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room: In existing public formal schools, where extra space is available, construction of an additional classroom and provision of a teacher will help in accommodating a number of new students.

- Enrolment of new students in new schools: In disadvantaged/remote areas where enrolment is low due to non-availability of formal public schools, construction of two-room new formal and opening of one room non formal schools will help in improving access to education.

- Targeted  Incentives: This strategy is aimed  at  retaining students, especially  those  from disadvantaged  groups,  especially  girls,  who  (mainly  due  to  financial  reasons)  drop  out  before completing primary school. These incentives (e.g. stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc.) will be given to students from poor families.

The key assumptions of  the  AJ&K Education Plan are:

- Using actual estimates of population for the year 2012 and net primary enrolment rates for 2011-12, projections for these variables were made for the base year 2012/13 and for the Plan period 2013-16;

- The Plan is based on annual physical targets and corresponding estimated costs for a three year period i.e. 2013-14-15-16;

- Under each strategy, a certain number of out-of-school children to be enrolled is specified;

- There are basically four types of schools assigned to absorb additional enrolment i.e., public sector formal schools; non-formal basic and feeder schools; religious or deeni madaris institutions; and private sector schools;

- Costs of additional enrolments are estimated for each type of school except private sector schools which are not financed by the public treasury;

- Under each strategy, besides specified development costs (i.e., Rs 1 million per additional room and Rs 3 million for a 2-room school in the public sector; and Rs 100,000 per new non formal school) , recurrent costs are estimated at the rate of  Rs 10,000 per student in formal public schools and Rs 5,000 per student in non-formal/feeder /deeni madrassah;

- Plan estimates are based on annual inflation rate of 10%;

- A student-teacher ratio of 30:1; and a teacher-administrator/supervisor ratio of 40:1 is assumed.

- For quality enhancement (e.g., teacher training, curriculum reform, provision of missing facilities, etc.), 20% of recurrent costs are added to the total cost.

- For innovative strategies (e.g., awareness campaigns to promote the importance of education, community involvement in supervision of local education; and use of media; etc.), an amount equivalent to 15% of recurrent costs plus quality enhancement costs is added to total costs.

- To improve access and retention, incentives such as stipends, food-for-education, uniforms, etc. will be targeted towards children in disadvantaged (rural/backward/remote) areas and belonging to disadvantaged groups (especially girls) in selected poor districts. These incentives are costed at Rs 3,000 per student for an equivalent of 15% of newly enrolled students.

- A contingency amount equivalent to 10% of all development, recurrent, quality and innovative strategies' costs is added to cover miscellaneous and unforeseen costs.

## Physical Targets and Cost Estimates for Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs
### Strategies to Accelerate New Enrolment and Enhance Quality in AJ&K

Within the Acceleration of Education-Related MDGs plan, a total number of 111,390 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in AJ&K, under four possible mutually exclusive strategies:

#### Strategy (a)

##### Enrolment of new students in existing schools (under-utilized/closed schools) (70% of all new enrolment)

A  major strategy to be implemented under the Plan suggests enrolment of out-of-school children in primary classes  of  existing  formal  and  non-formal  schools.  A  careful  assessment  of  schools  with  underutilized capacity as well as nonfunctional/closed schools will have to be undertaken before allocating new students to these institutions.

Under the strategy of enrolling new students in existing schools, a total number of 77,973 new students will be enrolled in primary classes in AJ&K during 2013-16. Of these, 40% will be enrolled in public formal and 10% in public non formal schools and 10% in deeni madaris and other schools. Private schools are estimated to absorb another 10% students (Table AS1a).

#### Strategy (b)

##### Enrolment of new students in formal schools through provision of an additional room (In 10% of existing schools)

This strategy is based on the construction of one additional classroom (estimated at a cost of Rs 1 million) and hiring  of  one  teacher  in  an  existing  public  school.  An  assessment  of  schools  with  extra  land  and possibility of enrolling out-of-school students will have to be carefully undertaken.

#### Strategy (d)

##### Targeted Incentives for access and retention for disadvantaged groups and girls (15% of all enrolments)

Of all primary enrolments (old and new), 15% of the students will be provided incentives for access and retention in primary schools. In AJ&K, 232,097 children will be targeted and provided incentives (equivalent to Rs. 3,000 per child) at a cost of Rs. 696 million (Tables ASd1 and ASd2).

## Implementation & Monitoring Mechanism

The implementation of the Plan will be the responsibility of the Provincial/Area governments. The Federal Government will however facilitate by providing a platform for inter provincial coordination, and to liaise with the donors and other stakeholders. Besides Federal Govt. may also provide technical and professional support to provinces and areas in planning, implementation and monitoring/evaluation, if required.

In each province/area, the implementation and monitoring mechanism for the  National Plan of Action will function at two levels:

### (i) Overall  Macro  Level  Inter-Governmental  Coordination:
This  will  ensure  close  coordination between  the  key  macro  players  i.e.,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Unit  (PCU),  the  Federal Government,  the  Provincial  Coordinating  Units  in  other  provinces/areas;  private  sector/  civil society and the international development partners.

For every year of the Plan, to review the progress and revision, if needed, the following steps are proposed.

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the first quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Secretaries of Education meeting after the second quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Focal Persons meeting after the third quarter of the Plan;

- Ÿ Inter-Provincial Education Ministers Conference at the end of every year of the Plan.

- Ÿ AEPAM will function as the Secretariat of the Federal Coordination Unit. (Updated  Progress  Reports  will  be  prepared  by  the  Provincial/Area  Coordination  Units  and discussed in the above fora / meetings)

### (ii) Intra-provincial Coordination and Implementation Network

The PCU and its various cells will not only provide the associated service at the macro level, i.e. with Federal Government, other provincial governments and international development partners, but will also undertake coordination and planning, data and research etc. at the provincial level as well as serve as an on-demand resource for district/local governments.

Each cell as well as the Unit will be staffed with personnel and technical support as and when identified by the provincial/area authorities.

### Monitoring and Evaluation Mechanism

The provinces and areas will establish and strengthen monitoring and evaluation mechanism to assess the implementation progress. For the purpose, monitoring and evaluation indicators will be developed and progress assessed. The monitoring indicators will cover financial allocation and expenditure against the plan requirement;  number  of  children  enrolled,  retained  and  completed  primary  education;  out-of-school monitoring and evaluation indicators will also include quality of education measurement.

Annual implementation progress reports against the Plan targets will be prepared by each province/area and shared with all concerned.  The yearly (annual) progress reports will also highlight the issues, challenges and bottlenecks in achieving the targets so that the said issues may be addressed. At the end (in June 2016) a comprehensive completion report will be prepared and shared.
